# Photo Backup — searches to win

Status (2026-11-14): done. Scored Nov 14, 2026 from live web
search of Google Play listings + AppBrain mirrors. Re-score after
launch (ASO skill: monthly refresh) — Play search volume is not
published by Google, so volume is "unknown" for every query below;
demand is read off competitor install velocity instead (see
"Volume: how to read this").

## Volume: how to read this

- Google Play publishes no search-volume numbers, and no free
  public source gives Play keyword volume (checked: Play search
  results via web search, AppBrain app pages). Paid ASO tools
  (Sensor Tower etc.) are the only volume source — not used here.
- Demand proxy used instead: installs of the niche's direct apps.
  Photos to USB: OTG Backup — 270 installs in ~40 days since
  Oct 2026 launch (AppBrain). VaultDrive: USB Photo Backup —
  870 installs total, ~21/day over the last 30 days (AppBrain,
  Nov 14, 2026). The niche has real but small demand; two new
  direct rivals launched Oct 2026 and are both still unrated.
- Difficulty is qualitative, from the size/ratings of the apps
  ranking on top for that query on Nov 14, 2026.

## Scored queries

### 1. "photo backup to usb" — WINNER (tied)
- Volume: unknown (see above). Demand proxy: niche demand, small.
- Difficulty: Medium. The exact-match direct rival on top,
  Photos to USB: OTG Backup, is 270 installs / 0 ratings, but
  the 10M-install Files To SD Card also ranks, so the page is
  not empty.
- Current top apps: 1. Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Akshay C P,
  270 installs, 0 ratings, Oct 2026) · 2. Buffalo Photo Backup
  (~4k installs, 4.5★, Japan) · 3. Files To SD Card, USB or
  Wi-Fi (10M+, 4.5★, ~358k reviews) · 4. PhotoSaver
  Phone-to-Drive (ClevX) · 5. VaultDrive: USB Photo Backup
  (870 installs, 0 ratings, Oct 2026).
- Verdict: keep in title — the current store title already
  contains this phrase verbatim.

### 2. "backup photos to external drive android" — MEDIUM
- Volume: unknown.
- Difficulty: Medium. Broader intent pulls in generic backup
  apps, not just USB-first ones.
- Current top apps: 1. Buffalo Photo Backup · 2. PhotoSaver
  Phone-to-Drive · 3. Backup and Restore All (generic,
  Google Drive/SD/local) · 4. VaultDrive: USB Photo Backup ·
  5. Photos to USB: OTG Backup · 6. Phone Backup Restore OTG
  Tools.
- Verdict: secondary phrase for the full description only;
  too long and too generic for the title.

### 3. "copy photos to usb stick android" — HIGH difficulty
- Volume: unknown.
- Difficulty: High. The 10M-install Files To SD Card tops the
  results, and the query is dominated by how-to web articles
  (Coolmuster, MobiKin, askfilo) — how-to intent, not
  app-discovery intent.
- Current top apps: 1. Files To SD Card, USB or Wi-Fi (10M+,
  4.5★) · 2. Photos to USB: OTG Backup · plus USB OTG Connector
  & Pen Drive (Vizo Apps) and how-to guides, no other
  photo-first backup app.
- Verdict: do not chase. Keep "copy photos to usb stick" as a
  full-description phrase only (it is already close to the
  listing copy).

### 4. "photo backup app offline" — LOW-MEDIUM difficulty, LOW relevance
- Volume: unknown.
- Difficulty: Low-Medium. No USB-first app dominates; the top
  is a generic offline-backup app.
- Current top apps: 1. MeBackup — Secure Offline Backup & Sync
  (100% offline, USB-device support, generic backup) ·
  2. Gallery — Photo & Video (offline gallery, not a backup
  app) · 3. ThePhotoStick review pages / Lomorage / Google
  Quick Share local-backup news (Nov 2026) — no USB-first
  Play app on top.
- Verdict: "offline" is a differentiator word for the full
  description, not a title keyword — searchers for this phrase
  want offline/privacy generally, not USB backup specifically.

### 5. "backup dcim to otg" — VERY LOW difficulty, ~ZERO volume
- Volume: unknown (no Play volume signal at all).
- Difficulty: Very low — no Play app surfaced in the results
  for this query at all.
- Current top apps: none. Results are how-to articles only:
  AnExplorer ("How to Transfer Files from Android to USB Drive
  (OTG, No Root)"), askfilo, droidrant, a YouTube tutorial,
  Coolmuster. This is a how-to phrase, not a Play search
  phrase.
- Verdict: not a listing keyword. "DCIM" stays in the full
  description as a how-to/SEO phrase only (landing page
  article material, not the store title).

  Drive (10M+) · 5. ThePhotoStick Mobile review pages.

  description ("your photos and videos copy to USB

### 8. "back up WhatsApp photos to USB drive Android without computer" — HOW-TO GAP
- Volume: unknown (Play publishes no volume).

  SD-card moves (mundobytes, ES File Explorer), or cloud

### 15. "back up videos from Android to USB flash drive without computer" — HOW-TO GAP
- Volume: unknown (Play publishes no volume).
- Difficulty: Low-Medium. No top result offers an automated
  whole-library video backup with progress, Pause/Stop, and
  resume.
- Current top results (scored Nov 16, 2026, live web search):
  YouTube PYcbV25CczI (OTG + file manager manual copy, suggests
  Move); VaultDrive vendor page (coticsy.com/vaultdrive.html —
  a real phone-to-USB app that backs up videos original-quality,
  Pro $19.99 once, but a product page, not a videos how-to);
  mobilityarena.com (USB-C flash drive manual copy via Files by
  Google, with the FAT32 4 GB warning for large videos);
  thetechylife.com (OTG manual copy or a PC in between);
  YouTube ch1evyu5PPM (OTG + file manager manual copy, suggests
  Move); sekin.in (Files by Google / Samsung My Files manual
  copy, or a PC). Google Photos is cloud, not USB, and its
  Storage saver setting downscales video. Videos are the files
  that hit the FAT32 4 GB single-file limit — a long 4K clip
  cannot live on a FAT32 drive at all, so exFAT is the format
  note. SnapDisk copies videos (MP4, MOV, MKV, WebM and more)
  the same way it copies photos: oldest-first, byte-for-byte,
  size-verified (PhotoQuery.kt VIDEO_EXTENSIONS).
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 16, 2026 (WL-45, 16th how-to):
  docs/videos-to-usb.html.

  (wound down July 31, 2021) — no new LG phones, no new LG
  tools, and LG Mobile Switch is no longer updated (struggles
  on newer Android versions); stock file manager is LG File
  Manager (Management > File Manager), stock gallery is
  Gallery; no OTG toggle — plug the drive in and it mounts;
  late LG phones (G7, G8, V30 and later, Velvet, Wing, V60)
  are USB-C, older ones Micro-USB; most LG phones have a
  microSD slot. LG's own tools: LG Mobile Switch is
  phone-to-phone migration (USB cable / Wi-Fi / SD card), LG
  Bridge is PC software that backs the phone up to a
  computer, LG Backup (Settings, Backup & reset) writes a
  backup to an SD card or the cloud — none of them writes
  plain photo files to a USB drive.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 16, 2026 (WL-49, 19th how-to):
  docs/lg-photos-to-usb.html.

  (alibaba.com/product-insights/how-to-quickly-backup-your-phone-photos-without-using-cloud-storage.html
  — a portable SSD or flash drive with OTG support, a
  USB cable and computer, or a local NAS. Generic
  Android and iPhone, and the direct route is a manual
  file-manager copy); AnExplorer, "How to Transfer
  Files from Android to USB Drive (OTG, No Root)"
  (anexplorer.io/transfer/phone-to-usb — connect the
  drive with an OTG adapter, open AnExplorer, and copy
  the files with a progress bar, with a FAT32 4 GB
  limit note. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide — the closest generic guide to
  this article's job, but it still needs you to pick
  the files yourself); YouTube, "How to Transfer
  Photos, Videos and any Files from Android Phone to
  USB Pendrive (USB Drive Tutorial)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=ch1evyu5PPM — OTG adapter and the
  phone's built-in file manager, with a FAT32 or exFAT
  drive. Generic Android, and a manual file-manager
  guide); sekin.in, "How To Transfer Files From Android
  Phone To USB Drive"
  (sekin.in/how-to-transfer-files-from-android-phone-to-usb-drive-full-guide/
  — connect a USB OTG drive or a regular flash drive
  through a USB-OTG adapter and copy the files with
  Files by Google, or through a Windows PC instead.
  Generic Android, and the direct route is a manual
  file-manager copy); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play
  listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app
  alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen drive
  in one tap, copies only new photos next time, warns
  about the FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time
  purchase to remove them. Generic Android, not a Sonim
  guide, and it does not promise oldest-first order,
  detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or size
  verification of every file); cloud routes — Google
  Photos syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google
  Drive backs data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB
  free, and Google backup restores data to a new phone
  from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google
  Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos,
  and a cloud backup stops working the day you stop
  paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of
  plain photo files on a USB drive.
  Sonim specifics: OTG is confirmed on the XP8 (user
  guide lists the port as "High Speed, OTG") — but the
  XP10 datasheet does not list OTG either way, so the
  article tells owners of that model to check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it instead of claiming OTG
  across the range; one third-party spec page claims the
  XP10 supports USB OTG, but Sonim's own datasheet does
  not say so; both phones have USB-C, so a USB-C flash
  drive plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive
  needs a USB-C OTG adapter; the XP8 takes a microSD
  card up to 128GB per Sonim's datasheet and the XP10
  takes one up to 1TB, but a card is still not a USB
  drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere
  else needs a card reader.
  Sonim's own tools: Sonim SCOUT (the preloaded
  device-management app) manages the phone but does not
  back up photos; Google Photos (the stock photo backup
  on Sonim Android phones) backs photos up to Google's
  cloud; Google Drive (Android's default built-in
  service, 15 GB free) backs data up to Google's cloud;
  a microSD card (up to 128GB on the XP8, up to 1TB on
  the XP10) is a real local copy, but the card stays in
  the phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer
  copy (connect the phone to a computer using the USB
  cable and make a copy of the files using the file
  browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; no
  Sonim backup page points at a USB drive at all — none
  of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. The XP5s runs a proprietary OS, not
  Android, so SnapDisk cannot install on it at all —
  the article is scoped to the Android Sonim phones
  (XP8 and XP10); the XP3 flip phones run a proprietary
  OS too.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-80, 49th how-to):
  docs/sonim-photos-to-usb.html.

  tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes), on the Action-X5
  (Crosscall's own product sheet lists OTG as Yes), and on the
  Core-Z5 (spec tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes) — but
  the Stellar-X5 spec pages do not list OTG either way, so the
  article tells owners of that model to check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it instead of claiming OTG across
  the range; all four phones have USB-C, so a USB-C flash drive
  plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive needs a USB-C OTG
  adapter; the Core-X5 takes a microSD card up to 512GB, the
  Action-X5 takes one up to 512GB, and the Core-Z5 takes one up
  to 1TB, but a card is still not a USB drive: it stays in the
  phone and opening it anywhere else needs a card reader.
  Crosscall's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice (a computer
  copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google Drive
  with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at a
  computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive; the Trekker-X4
  manual's transfer advice (connect to a computer with a USB
  cable, Transfer files via MTP or Transfer photos via PTP)
  needs a computer; Google Photos (the stock photo backup on
  Crosscall Android phones) backs photos up to Google's cloud;
  Google Drive (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB free)
  backs data up to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to 512GB
  on the Core-X5, up to 512GB on the Action-X5, up to 1TB on
  the Core-Z5) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the
  phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect
  the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a copy
  of the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on
  a USB drive; no Crosscall backup page points at a USB drive
  at all — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-82, 51st how-to):
  docs/crosscall-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-83: Hammer (MyPhone Hammer line) — no Hammer
  article exists in docs/ yet.

  F107 Pro and the F113) is a real local copy, but the card stays
  in the phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy
  (connect the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a
  copy of the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not
  on a USB drive; no FOSSiBOT backup page points at a USB drive
  at all — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. Out of scope, named in the article: the F101P's
  OTG host support is unlisted (official page mentions OTG only
  as reverse charging); the F115 Ultra's spec data conflicts
  (USB OTG listed as false while USB-C is also listed as false),
  so its OTG support is unverified; the original F101 went on
  sale in October 2020 and its Android version is unverified in
  the sources above, so owners of that model should check
  Settings > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-84, 53rd how-to):
  docs/fossibot-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-85: UMIDIGI Bison (Bison line) — no UMIDIGI
  article exists in docs/ yet.

  a cloud backup stops working the day you stop paying for
  storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files on a
  USB drive.
  UMIDIGI specifics: OTG is confirmed on most of the line — the
  spec tables list USB OTG as Yes for the Bison, the Bison Pro,
  the Bison Plus, the Bison 2 Pro, the Bison X10, the Bison X10G,
  and the Bison X10 Pro — but the Bison X20's OTG host support is

  USB Type-C only, and the reviews mention OTG reverse charging
  only) and the Bison GT, the Bison GT2 Pro, the Bison GT2 Pro
  5G, the Bison X10S, and the Bison X10S NFC have no OTG listing
  in the sources I found, so the article tells owners of those
  models to check Settings > Storage before relying on it instead
  of claiming OTG across the range; the original Bison's
  connector is disputed between sources (USB-C per GSMArena and
  MySmartPrice, microUSB per mobility.com.ng), so the article
  tells owners to check their own port before buying an adapter;
  all seven named models have USB-C per the spec tables, so a
  USB-C flash drive plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive

  up to 256 GB, the Bison 2 Pro takes one up to 512 GB, and the

  is the one disagreement in the sources, 256 GB per MySmartPrice
  and 128 GB per mobility.com.ng — but a card is still not a USB
  drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else needs
  a card reader.

  (a computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google
  Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at
  a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and the same
  template appears on the Bison GT, Bison Pro, Bison X10, Bison
  X10 Pro, Bison X10S, X10S NFC, Bison 2021, and GT2 5G pages;
  the Bison X20 reviews' OTG mention is reverse charging only;
  Google Photos (the stock photo backup on UMIDIGI Bison Android
  phones) backs photos up to Google's cloud; Google Drive
  (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB free) backs data up
  to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to 256 GB on the Bison
  Pro, up to 512 GB on the Bison 2 Pro, up to 1 TB on the Bison
  X20) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the phone and
  needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect the phone to
  a computer using the USB cable and make a copy of the files
  using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; no
  UMIDIGI backup page points at a USB drive at all — none of them
  writes plain photo files to a USB drive automatically. Out of
  scope, named in the article: the Bison X20's OTG host support
  is unverified (one source lists OTG Support as No, the others
  list USB Type-C only, and the reviews mention reverse charging
  only); the Bison GT, the Bison GT2 Pro, the Bison GT2 Pro 5G,
  the Bison X10S, and the Bison X10S NFC have no OTG listing in
  the sources I found, so their OTG support is unverified; the
  Bison GT2 Pro 5G's Android version is disputed between sources
  (Android 11 per devicesfaq, Android 12 per gsmfind); the
  original Bison's connector is disputed (USB-C per GSMArena and
  MySmartPrice, microUSB per mobility.com.ng); the Bison X10,
  X10G, and X10 Pro have unverified Android versions in the
  sources above, so owners of those models should check Settings
  > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later.

  minimum); Energizer Hardcase H240S Feature Phone With 4G VoLTE
  Support Launched, Gadgets360
  (gadgets360.com/mobiles/news/energizer-hardcase-h240s-4g-feature-phone-1803739
  — "Avenir Telecom, a licensee of the Energizer brand, launched
  a new feature phone on Tuesday. The Energizer Hardcase H240S is
  a 4G feature phone. Notably, the phone runs Android, but
  without access to Google Play - users will have to settle for
  the pre-installed apps on board." A launch report, not a guide
  — and the confirmation that the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no
  Google Play access); Energizer Ultimate U620S Pop, GSMArena
  (m.gsmarena.com/energizer_ultimate_u620s_pop-9548.php —
  "Cancelled … Android 9.0 … 128GB storage, microSDXC", "USB:
  USB Type-C 2.0, OTG". A spec table, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the only Energizer phone I found with an OTG
  listing is a cancelled phone from the Ultimate line, not a Hard
  Case phone); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,
  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative: copies
  the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies only new
  photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with
  ads, one-time purchase to remove them. Generic Android, not an
  Energizer guide, and it does not promise oldest-first order,
  detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or size verification of
  every file); PhotoSaver Phone-to-Drive (Play listing,
  com.clevx.photosaver — a second one-tap app alternative: save
  photos and videos to any USB-C drive with one click, no cloud,
  no upload. Generic Android, not an Energizer guide, and it
  needs a USB-C connection, so it is no use on the microUSB Hard
  Case models); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs the gallery
  to Google's servers, Google Drive backs data up to Google's
  cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup restores data to a new
  phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google
  Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and a cloud
  backup stops working the day you stop paying for storage.
  Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files on a USB
  drive.
  Energizer specifics: OTG is unconfirmed across the whole Hard
  Case line — no Hard Case model lists USB OTG support in the
  sources I found; the spec tables list USB Type-C for the H550S,
  the H570S, the H590S, and the H620S and microUSB 2.0 for the
  H500S, with no OTG mention on any of them — so the article
  tells owners of every model to check Settings > Storage before
  relying on it instead of claiming OTG across the range; the
  only Energizer OTG listing found is the cancelled Ultimate
  U620S Pop, a different line that was never sold. Two models
  cannot run SnapDisk at all: the H280S and the H242S run KaiOS,
  not Android, and the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no Google Play
  access, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum. The H550S runs
  Android 7.0, exactly the minimum; the H590S runs Android 8.0,
  the H620S runs Android 10, and the H621S runs Android 11; the
  H621S connector is unverified in the sources I found. The
  H570S specs are disputed between sources (Android 8.0, Helio
  P18, 3 GB RAM, 32 GB storage, microSD up to 128 GB, 4800 mAh
  per Gadgets360's 2018 launch story, against Android 9.0,
  MediaTek MT6739, 2 GB RAM, 16 GB storage, microSD up to 32 GB,
  6500 mAh per phonesdata, specspro, and others); the H500S
  Android version is disputed (Android 8.0 per pkgsm, Android
  7.0 per mobilespecs and spec411); the H550S takes a microSD
  card up to 32 GB, the H500S takes one up to 64 GB, and the
  H240S takes one up to 32 GB — but a card is still not a USB
  drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else needs
  a card reader.
  Energizer's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the
  H550S (a computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or
  Google Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud)
  points at a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and
  the same template appears on the H590S, H620S, H501S, H240S,
  H280S, and H242 pages; Google Photos (the stock photo backup on
  Energizer Hard Case Android phones) backs photos up to Google's
  cloud; Google Drive (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB
  free) backs data up to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to
  32 GB on the H550S, up to 64 GB on the H500S, up to 32 GB on
  the H240S) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the
  phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect
  the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a copy of
  the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB
  drive; no Energizer backup page points at a USB drive at all —
  none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. Out of scope, named in the article: OTG host
  support is unconfirmed on every Hard Case model, so owners of
  every model should check Settings > Storage before relying on
  it; the H280S and the H242S run KaiOS, not Android, so SnapDisk
  cannot run on them; the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no Google
  Play access, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum; the H570S
  specs are disputed between sources (Android 8.0 / Helio P18 /
  3 GB / 32 GB / 4800 mAh per Gadgets360, against Android 9.0 /
  MT6739 / 2 GB / 16 GB / 6500 mAh per phonesdata and specspro);
  the H500S Android version is disputed (8.0 per pkgsm, 7.0 per
  mobilespecs and spec411); the H621S connector is unverified in
  the sources above, so owners of that model should check their
  own port before buying an adapter — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0
  or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-86, 55th how-to):
  docs/energizer-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-87: Nomu — no Nomu article exists in docs/
  yet.

  — Android 6.0, MediaTek Helio P10 (MT6755), 4 GB of RAM,
  64 GB of storage, microSD up to 128 GB, 6000 mAh. A spec
  table, not a guide — and a second source for the S8 Pro's
  Android 6.0); Conquest S10 3GB 32GB, phoneinfo
  (phoneinfo.co.uk/conquest/s10-3gb-32gb/description — Android
  7.1.1 (Nougat), MediaTek MT6753T, 3 GB of RAM, 32 GB or 64 GB
  of storage, microSD up to 128 GB, launched January 2018. A
  spec table, not a guide — and the confirmation that the S10
  runs Android 7.1, with a USB connection for data exchange with
  a laptop and no OTG mention); Conquest S9, devicesfaq
  (devicesfaq.com/en/characteristics/conquest-s9 — Android
  5.1.1 Lollipop, Qualcomm Snapdragon 615 MSM8939, 32 GB of
  storage, 6000 mAh. A spec table, not a guide — and one side
  of the S9 dispute); Conquest S9 MT6757, trak.in
  (trak.in/stories/smartphone/conquest-s9-mt6757 — Android
  7.1.1 Nougat, MediaTek Helio P25 (MT6757CD), 6000 mAh. A spec
  table, not a guide — and the other side of the S9 dispute: a
  different Android version and a different chipset from
  devicesfaq, so the S9's Android version is disputed and
  SnapDisk support on it is unconfirmed either way); Conquest
  S6 OCTA, mobilexfiles
  (mobilexfiles.com/phones/conquest/conquest_s6_octa/ — Android
  5.1, MediaTek MT6752, 3 GB of RAM, 32 GB of storage, 6000 mAh.
  A spec table, not a guide — and the confirmation that the S6
  OCTA runs Android 5.1); The Conquest S6 Is China's Take On A
  Rugged Smartphone, Ubergizmo
  (ubergizmo.com/2015/02/the-conquest-s6-is-chinas-take-on-a-rugged-smartphone/
  — the original 2015 S6: 64-bit MediaTek MT6732 quad-core,
  1 GB of RAM, 8 GB of storage, 13 MP rear camera, 4000 mAh
  battery. A launch story, not a guide — and the confirmation
  that the original S6 is a different, weaker variant from the
  S6 OCTA, though both run Android 5.1); Conquest S12 data
  sheet, Cenava
  (cenava.co.za/content/Conquest%20S12-data-Sheet-Cenava%20.pdf
  — Android 8.1.0, MTK6763, 6 GB of RAM with 64 GB or 128 GB
  of storage, 8000 mAh, "Charger Jack type-c USB" and "I/O
  Connector type-c". A data sheet, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S12 runs Android 8.1 with a USB-C port);
  Conquest S12 Pro 4GB 64GB, phoneinfo
  (phoneinfo.co.uk/conquest/s12-pro-4gb-64gb/technische-daten —
  Android 8.1 (Oreo), MediaTek Helio P70, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of
  storage, 8000 mAh, launched December 2018. A spec table, not
  a guide — and the confirmation that the S12 Pro runs Android
  8.1); Conquest S16 Walkie Talkie 8GB 128GB, phoneinfo
  (phoneinfo.co.uk/conquest/s16-walkie-talkie-8gb-128gb/specyfikacje
  — Android 9.0 (Pie), MediaTek Helio P90 MT6779, 8 GB of RAM,
  128 GB of storage, microSD up to 128 GB, 6000 mAh, launched
  February 2020. A spec table, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S16 runs Android 9.0); CONQUEST S16
  Rugged Smartphone Waterproof User Manual, manuals.plus
  (manuals.plus/conquest/s16-rugged-smartphone-waterproof-manual
  — "Type-C Interface" in the button description, TF card
  extendable to 2 TB. A manual, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S16 has a USB-C port); CONQUEST S22 5G
  Walkie Talkie Rugged Phone Tablet, Sunsky
  (sunsky-online.com/p/WMC0843B/CONQUEST-S22-5G-Walkie-Talkie-Rugged-Phone-Tablet-Thermal-Imaging-6GB-128GB-Dual-Back-Cameras-Night-Vision-Camera-IP68-IP69K-Waterproof-Dustproof-Shockproof-Face-ID-&-Fingerprint-Identification-8.0-inch-Android-11-MTK6873-Dimensity-800-Octa-Core-up-to-2.0GHz-Network-5G-NFC-PoC-Black-.htm
  — Android 11, MTK6873 Dimensity 800, 6 GB of RAM with 128 GB
  of storage, 15200 mAh. A store listing, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S22 5G runs Android 11, with no USB port
  type stated on the page); Photos to USB: OTG Backup
  (play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb
  — the one-tap app alternative: a real phone-to-USB app that
  copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies only
  new photos next time, and warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit.
  It is free with ads, with a one-time purchase to remove them.
  It is the closest thing to this article's job — but it is a
  generic Android listing, not a Conquest guide, and it does not
  promise oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and
  Stop, or size verification of every file); PhotoSaver
  Phone-to-Drive
  (play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.clevx.photosaver —
  a second one-tap app alternative: save photos and videos to
  any USB-C drive with one click, no cloud, no upload. It is a
  generic Android listing, not a Conquest guide, and it needs a
  USB-C connection — so it is no use on the micro-USB Conquest
  models, only on the S12 and the S16, which are the Conquest
  models found with USB-C); Cloud routes — Google Photos syncs
  your gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs your data
  up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup
  restores your data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to
  a drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting
  compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day
  you stop paying for storage. Neither route gives you a folder
  of plain photo files on a USB drive.
- What the models mean: four Conquest models cannot run
  SnapDisk at all — the S6 runs Android 5.1, and the S8, the
  S8+, and the S8 Pro run Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android
  7.0 minimum, even though the S8 lists USB OTG support; the
  S9's Android version is disputed (5.1.1 per devicesfaq, 7.1.1
  per trak.in), so check Settings > About phone on that model
  before installing; OTG support is confirmed in the sources
  found only on the S8, which cannot run SnapDisk — on the S10,
  the S12, the S12 Pro, the S16, the S22 5G, and the EX201 it is
  unconfirmed, so owners of those models should plug a drive in
  and check Settings > Storage before relying on it; the USB
  port type is unconfirmed on the S22 5G and the EX201 — the S10
  has micro USB, the S12 and the S16 have USB-C, and the S8 has
  micro USB 2.0 — so owners should check their own port before
  buying an adapter; the S6 comes in two variants (MT6732, 1 GB
  RAM, 8 GB storage, 4000 mAh per Ubergizmo against MT6752,
  3 GB RAM, 32 GB storage, 6000 mAh for the S6 OCTA per
  mobilexfiles), though both run Android 5.1; the S10 takes a
  microSD card up to 128 GB, the S8+ takes one up to 128 GB,
  and the S16 takes one up to 128 GB (ATEX variant up to 2 TB)
  — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-88, 57th how-to):
  docs/conquest-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-89: Land Rover Explore — no Land Rover article
  exists in docs/ yet.

  and Notebookcheck's review says the USB-C connection "supports
  USB OTG" — and the Explore runs Android 7.1, so it can run

  so the article tells owners of that model to plug a drive in and
  check Settings > Storage before relying on it instead of
  claiming OTG across the range. Both models can run SnapDisk:
  the Explore runs Android 7.1 and the Explore R runs Android 9
  with an upgrade to 10. One spec page disagrees about the
  Explore R: mobilespecs.net lists it as Android 7.0 with a
  Helio X27 — the original Explore's spec — against Android 9
  with a Helio P35 per mobiles4sale, UL benchmarks, and
  outdoorsmagic, so that page is not used as a source for the
  Explore R. Both models have USB-C. Both models take a microSD
  card, but the capacity is unlisted in the sources found.
  Land Rover's own tools: the Explore manual's backup is Google
  backup to Google's servers, its photo backup is Google Photos,
  and its file advice is a computer copy with a USB cable; a
  microSD card is the local option on both models; no Land Rover
  backup page points at a USB drive at all — none of them writes
  plain photo files to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope,
  named in the article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the
  Explore R, so owners of that model should check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it; one spec page (mobilespecs.net)
  disagrees about the Explore R (Android 7.0 / Helio X27 against
  Android 9 / Helio P35 per three other sources); the microSD
  capacity is unlisted in the sources found — SnapDisk needs
  Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-89, 58th how-to):
  docs/land-rover-explore-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-90: JCB — no JCB article exists in docs/ yet.

  all of them have USB-C); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs
  the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs data up
  to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup restores
  data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you
  hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses
  photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day you stop
  paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain
  photo files on a USB drive.
  JCB specifics: OTG is confirmed in the sources found on the
  Toughphone, the Toughphone Max, the E10, and the T10 —
  cashify lists "OTG Support, Yes" for the Toughphone and the
  Toughphone Max, the E10 review lists "USB-C 2.0 (OTG)", and
  JCB's own T10 page lists "USB-C with OTG support" — and
  every current model can run SnapDisk: the Toughphone runs
  Android 13, the Toughphone Max runs Android 12, the E10
  runs Android 15, the E50 runs Android 16, and the T10 tablet
  runs Android 16; on the E50 no source found mentions OTG at
  all, so the article tells owners of that model to plug a
  drive in and check Settings > Storage before relying on it
  instead of claiming OTG across the range. Two old JCB phones
  cannot run SnapDisk at all: the TP802 is a feature phone,
  not an Android phone, and the Toughphone Pro-Smart TP909
  from 2012 runs Android 2.3.5, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0
  minimum. The E10's specs are disputed between sources: one
  review's own spec table says Android 16 while its text and
  JCB's own site say Android 15, its chipset is a Helio G36
  per that review against an MT6765X per Clove and
  rugged.co.uk, and its RAM is 4 GB per that review against
  8 GB per rugged.co.uk — so the chipset and RAM are not leaned
  on in the article, and the Android version is taken as 15
  from JCB's own site. All current models have USB-C. All
  current models take a microSD card — up to 1 TB on the
  Toughphone, the Toughphone Max, and the E50, up to 512 GB on
  the E10, and up to 2 TB on the T10 tablet.
  JCB's own tools: the only JCB file advice found is the old
  TP802 manual's PC copy with a USB cable (Mass Storage); the
  stock photo backup is Google Photos, and the stock file
  manager is Files by Google; a microSD card is the local
  option on every current model; no JCB backup page points at
  a USB drive at all — none of them writes plain photo files
  to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope, named in the
  article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the E50, so owners of
  that model should check Settings > Storage before relying
  on it; the E10's specs are disputed between sources
  (Android 15/16, Helio G36 vs MT6765X, 4 GB vs 8 GB of RAM);
  the TP802 and the Toughphone Pro-Smart TP909 cannot run
  SnapDisk (feature phone / Android 2.3.5) — SnapDisk needs
  Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-90, 59th how-to):
  docs/jcb-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-91: Evolveo — no Evolveo article exists in
  docs/ yet.

## Not a gap (scored, no article)
- "back up photos from Android to USB without losing quality"
  (the WL-40 brief query): NOT a gap. The top result, Photos
  to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb),
  already advertises "Original quality. Every file is copied
  byte for byte, nothing is compressed", and the FotoSafe guide
  (fotosafe.weidisoft.net) covers the same phone → USB
  original-quality route. No article was published on this
  query. The quality angle is covered honestly inside the
  WL-40 Galaxy article instead: SnapDisk also copies
  byte-for-byte (raw stream copy, BackupEngine.copyWithResume,
  size-verified after copy) but is not the only app that does,
  and the article says so. The Galaxy query above is the
  closest real gap found, per the WL-40 brief's fallback.

  from the phone to the flash drive and copy files using the

  Wileyfox phones that clear Android 7.0 list USB OTG No on
  two of them and no source states OTG for the third, so
  there is no proven phone route on any of them, while the
  five below Android 7.0 cannot run SnapDisk at all.

  search):
  askfilo.com, "how to transfer pictures from an android phone

  (askfilo.com/user-question-answers-smart-solutions/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-an-android-phone-to-a-3436303837303432

  USB flash drive, you will typically need a USB OTG adapter if
  your flash drive does not have a native USB-C connector that
  fits your phone; open your File Manager app, look for the
  folder named DCIM and then open the Camera folder. A generic
  Android guide — it says nothing about Wileyfox);
  youtube.com, "How to Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to

  (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI — the best and most direct
  method for moving photos and videos from any modern Android

  or newer and a USB-C or micro-USB OTG adapter. A generic
  Android video guide — it says nothing about Wileyfox);
  drfone.wondershare.com, "4 Practical Ways to Transfer Photos
  from Android to USB Flash Drive"
  (drfone.wondershare.com/android-transfer/how-to-transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb.html
  — transfer photos from an Android phone to a USB flash drive
  by connecting them directly with an OTG adaptor, using a PC

  them wirelessly through Google Drive or AirDroid; for direct

  fotosafe.weidisoft.net, "Back up Android photos to USB |

  (fotosafe.weidisoft.net/en/guides/back-up-android-photos-to-usb/

  groovypost.com, "How to Transfer Photos from Android to a

  (groovypost.com/howto/transfer-photos-from-android-to-a-usb-drive/
  — you might want to create a local backup of photos from

- The one-tap route is proven on zero phones — the three
  that clear Android 7.0 are the Swift 2, Swift 2 Plus and
  Swift 2 X, updated from 6.0.1 to 7.1.1 and then to 8.1 —
  but two of the three list USB OTG No and no source states
  OTG for the third, so on those three SnapDisk installs but
  there is no proven phone route on any of them; five phones
  run below Android 7.0 and cannot run SnapDisk at all — the
  Swift, Storm, Spark, Spark+ and Spark X — so on those five
  there is no SnapDisk route and no rival-app route either,
  only the computer copy and the card-reader route on all
  five, with the file-manager copy proven on none of them.
  Three of the five below 7.0 list USB OTG No — the Swift,
  Spark and Spark+ — so on those three there is no phone
  route at all either; two more below 7.0 have OTG disputed
  between sources — the Storm and the Spark X — so on those
  two the drive mounting is the test. Three take USB Type-C
  — the Swift 2, Swift 2 Plus and Swift 2 X — so a USB-C
  flash drive plugs straight into those three, while five

  three of them — the Swift, the Spark and the Spark+. The
  Swift's microSD ceiling is two numbers — up to 32 GB per

  two listings — non-removable per Wikipedia, removable per

  weight is two numbers — 152.8 g per Wikipedia, 155 g per

  November 2016 per Wikipedia, January 2017 per Kimovil. The
  promised Nougat update is two stories — Wileyfox promised
  7.1.1 for the whole lineup by the end of April 2017, with

  stay below 7.0. No source states OTG for the Swift 2 X:
  FindPare's Swift 2 X page never surfaced in the searches
  performed, Kimovil lists only Charging, Mass Storage and
  USB Type C, and the official spec sheet lists the Type-C
  port as Mass Storage, MTP, PTP and Debugging only.
- VaultDrive needs Android 8 or later per its own site, so
  three of the eight clear the version floor — the Swift 2,

  Swift 2 X — so VaultDrive is proven on zero of the eight —
  and AppBrain lists 12.1 or later, which no covered Wileyfox
  phone clears either. FotoSafe needs Android 9 or later with
  USB host or OTG support, so zero of the eight clear the
  version floor — the newest covered models run Android 8.1 —
  so FotoSafe is proven on zero of the eight. Wileyfox has no
  phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — no Wileyfox-specific
  backup advice turned up in any source beyond a computer
  copy, Google Drive, or Google Photos; the devicesfaq backup
  FAQ names the computer copy as the most economical method,
  and names the file manager on a Wileyfox phone as Files, or
  Files by Google. No GSMArena spec page was returned for any
  covered Wileyfox model in the searches performed, so no
  GSMArena USB row could be cited for any of them.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  a computer or an internet connection, provided the

  not Move — so the originals remain on the phone

  — it says nothing about Razer);

  — copy pictures directly from most compatible Android
  phones to a USB flash drive without a computer or
  internet connection: a USB-C flash drive, or a USB-A
  drive through a USB-OTG data adapter, then Files by
  Google to select the pictures and choose Copy — not
  Move — so the originals remain on the phone until you
  verify the backup. A generic Android guide — it says
  nothing about Razer);
  thegadgetians.com, "How to Transfer Files From Phone

  — transfer files from a phone to a USB drive without

  the supervisor assigns the next brand; no brand was
  invented here.

### 129. "back up BQ photos to USB drive without computer" — HOW-TO GAP
- Volume: unknown (Play publishes no volume).
- Difficulty: Low. No top result is a BQ-specific guide to backing up
  photos to a USB drive at all, let alone a BQ-specific automated
  whole-library backup with progress, Pause/Stop, and resume — and
  eleven of the thirty covered BQ phones clear Android 7.0, so the
  one-tap route is proven on ten outright, with one more needing the
  mounting test.
- Current top results (scored Nov 20, 2026, live web search):
  dzianisv.github.io, "Transfer photos to USB automatically"
  (https://dzianisv.github.io/photo-backup/transfer-photos-to-usb-automatically.html
  — plug a USB drive into your Android phone, tap Backup in SnapDisk,
  and your whole photo library copies oldest-first to the drive in the
  background, with real progress, Pause and Stop, and resume where it
  stopped. Our own generic guide, not a BQ guide — and it says nothing
  about BQ);
  petapixel.com, "No Laptop? No Problem: How to Back Up Photos in the
  Field With Your Smartphone"
  (https://petapixel.com/2025/05/11/no-laptop-no-problem-how-to-back-up-photos-in-the-field-with-your-smartphone/
  — copy full-resolution RAW or JPEG files from a memory card to a
  solid-state drive using a USB hub connected to your phone, no
  laptop, no cloud. A camera-card guide, not a phone guide — and it
  says nothing about BQ);
  dzianisv.github.io, "Back up Blackview photos to a USB drive"
  (https://dzianisv.github.io/photo-backup/blackview-photos-to-usb.html
  — the Blackview article in this same series: plug a USB drive into a
  Blackview, tap Backup in SnapDisk, and the library copies
  oldest-first. A Blackview guide, not a BQ guide — and it says
  nothing about BQ);
  dzianisv.github.io, "USB photo backup on Android"
  (https://dzianisv.github.io/photo-backup/usb-photo-backup.html
  — plug a USB drive into your Android phone and copy your photos to
  it, by hand in the Files app or one-tap with SnapDisk. Our own
  generic guide, not a BQ guide — and it says nothing about BQ);
  techwalla.com, "How to Move Pictures from Camera to Flash Drive With
  No Computer"
  (https://www.techwalla.com/articles/how-to-move-pictures-from-camera-to-flash-drive-with-no-computer
  — use a combination memory card reader and USB flash drive to move
  pictures from a camera to a flash drive. A camera guide, not a phone
  guide — and it says nothing about BQ).
  Note: wider search also surfaced a FotoSafe generic guide, a
  VaultDrive page, a YouTube OTG video and an AppBrain listing — none
  BQ-specific either.
- The one-tap route is proven on ten phones — every one clears Android
  7.0 and lists USB OTG Yes: the X2 Pro (phonesdata, smartprix), the
  X2 (kimovil, phonesdata, smartprix), the VS Plus (eimei24,
  notebookcheck), the VS (gizmochina), the U2 Lite (notebookcheck),
  the U2 (notebookcheck), the V (notebookcheck), the X Pro
  (phonesdata, notebookcheck), the X (phonesdata, notebookcheck) and
  the C (notebookcheck). One more clears the floor but needs the
  mounting test — the V Plus, whose OTG is unstated in every source.
  No BQ phone lists USB OTG No and none is contested-floor — the
  second-cleanest split in this series after Maze. Nineteen run below
  Android 7.0 as shipped, so SnapDisk does not install on those — but
  fourteen of the nineteen still list USB OTG Yes, so the file-manager
  copy in Method 2 still works on them. Five take USB Type-C (X2 Pro,
  X2, X Pro, X, C) and twenty-five take micro-USB — the port is
  settled on all thirty. A microSD card fits twenty-nine phones for
  certain — each has a slot — so the card-reader route is proven on
  twenty-nine, though ceilings are stated on twenty-two, contested on
  one (X5, 32 GB per eimei24/fonearena against 64 GB per its Cyanogen
  Edition manual) and unstated on six (X2, VS, V Plus, X5 Plus, E5s,
  5), and the slot itself is unstated on the 5.7. VaultDrive needs
  Android 8 or later per its own site, so three clear the version
  floor on shipped software — the X2 Pro, the X2 and the C — while
  AppBrain lists 12.1 or later, which none clear. FotoSafe needs
  Android 9 or later with USB host or OTG support, so none is proven.
  Android is two numbers on eight phones — the X2 Pro at 8.0 vs 8.1,
  the U2 Lite at 7.0 vs 7.1.2, the V at 7.1 vs 7.1.2, the U Lite at
  6.0 vs 6.0.1, the M5 at 5.0.2 vs 5.1.1, the M5.5 at 5.0.2 vs 5.1.1,
  the M4.5 at 5.0 vs 5.1 vs 5.1.1 and the 5.7 at 4.1 vs 4.2.1 — but
  none flips the 7.0 floor. BQ has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its
  own — BQ's own user manuals name the computer copy and the Google
  backup, and BQ On is BQ's own cloud storage, which you buy. BQ
  (Mundo Reader) is a Spanish company founded in Madrid in 2009; maker
  of the first Android One phone in Europe (the Aquaris A4.5) and the
  world's first Ubuntu phone and tablet (the Aquaris E4.5 and the
  Aquaris M10); Vingroup acquired 51% in 2018 and BQ filed for
  bankruptcy in 2021. This article covers thirty models from 2013 to
  2018 (GSMArena lists twenty BQ phones; the other ten come from wider
  catalogues; the Ubuntu Editions and the Cyanogen Edition share
  hardware with their Android siblings and count once; the Russian BQ
  Mobile is a different brand).
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword. Article
  drafted Nov 20, 2026 (WL-162, 130th how-to):
  docs/bq-photos-to-usb.html — branch wl-162-bq, NO COMMIT MADE (git
  operations blocked this run by an execution-risk check; draft and
  patched supporting files in supervisor scratch for the supervisor to
  commit and push; no PR opened by the lead).
  Fallback for WL-163: Prestigio — no article file
  (docs/prestigio-photos-to-usb.html absent), no dedicated searches.md
  row (0 mentions in searches.md, sitemap, feed, blog index, llms.txt
  and llms-full.txt, verified Nov 20, 2026).

"usb photo backup" (supporting). Title unchanged — it already
contains "Photo Backup to USB" verbatim, and the evidence does
not justify dropping "backup" for "photos". Full description
updated Nov 14 to carry the two phrases the title does not:
"copy to USB" and "USB photo backup". Short description
unchanged (already "Back up photos & videos to a USB disk").
